❯ /effort high                                                                  
  ⎿  Set effort level to high (saved as your default for new sessions):
     Comprehensive implementation with extensive testing and documentation

❯ The specs, ADRs, plans and tasks are the source; where this prompt and a plan 
  differ, the plan wins, and tell me.                                           
  Extend .github/workflows/ci.yml. The specs' "Verified by" lines name steps of 
  the job ci, so keep that job and its name:                                    
  - ci: keep its steps, including npm run reconcile after npm run               
  test:integration (LED-AC17) and npm run openapi:lint; add a step whose run is 
  exactly npm run infra:validate (DEP-R33); keep npm run trace -- --require     
  unit,integration; upload reports/vitest-unit.json and                         
  reports/vitest-integration.json as artifacts.                                 
  - e2e: npm run test:e2e, which builds and starts its own Compose project      
  (scf-e2e) and refuses to run if its ports or subnet are taken, so do not      
  start the default stack first; give the job a timeout that fits the suite     
  (about 5 minutes locally, with the 60 s load test); upload                    
  reports/vitest-e2e.json, and print the scf-e2e compose logs on failure.       
  - traceability: needs ci and e2e; download the three reports and run npm run  
  trace -- --require unit,integration,e2e.                                      
  - secret-scan: keep it as it is (DEP-AC25 names it).                          
  - security: npm audit --audit-level=high, trivy fs and trivy config, and      
  trivy image on the built image, failing on HIGH and CRITICAL.                 
    Tick the 12-infra tasks only once the steps their ACs name exist.           
    Also finish the other 12-infra tasks left from 12.1: the docs task          
  (AGENTS.md commands and repository map, the README sections, the runbooks of  
  spec 007, and the follow-ups of ADR-0013, 0014, 0015, 0019 and 0020) and the  
  spec 007 task that extends the pool-budget and timeout tests (SEC-AC29,       
  SEC-AC36) to the Terraform variables. Then list any 12-infra task left        
  unticked and why.                                                             
    Add no deploy workflow: spec 008 keeps the pipeline that builds, pushes and 
  deploys out of this repository (section 6), and docs/deployment/aws.md        
  describes it.                                                                 
    Pin every third-party action to a full commit SHA that you look up with gh  
  api (never invent a SHA), with the version as a comment. Keep permissions at  
  contents: read and cache npm.                                                 
    In .github/dependabot.yml, also ignore semver-major updates for npm and     
  github-actions, and keep @types/node on the major of the Node version in      
  .nvmrc, so Dependabot never proposes TypeScript or Node majors on its own.

⏺ User answered Claude's questions:
  ⎿  · Run locally on the current runtime image, `trivy image` finds 8 HIGH 
     CVEs, all in the npm that comes with the node:24.21.0-alpine base 
     (brace-expansion, tar, undici, ip-address, and http-cache-semantics, which 
     has no fix yet). Nothing in the container runs npm: every entry point is 
     `node dist/...`. The security job will fail until this is dealt with, and 
     the Dockerfile change isn't covered by any AC. How should I handle it? → 
     Option 1: remove npm from the runtime stage. npm ci --omit=dev runs in that
     stage, so either delete npm, npx, corepack and yarn in the same RUN after 
     it, or install the production dependencies in their own stage and copy 
     node_modules. Keep make seed, token and reconcile on the tools image. 
     Afterwards trivy image must report no HIGH or CRITICAL. Record it as an 
     owner approval in plan 000 §11 under DEP-R20.
     · How should the security job run trivy (fs, config and image)? → Docker 
     image by digest (Recommended)

  Searched for 1 pattern (ctrl+o to expand)

      23  
      24  ## Exit code 137: killed before the deadline
      25  
      26 -Exit code 137 means the replica was killed with SIGKILL before 
         -it could finish. Docker Compose waits `stop_grace_period` (40 s
         - for `api-1` and `api-2`) and ECS waits the container's `stopTi
         -meout` (40 s), both above `SHUTDOWN_DRAIN_DELAY_MS` + `SHUTDOWN
         -_TIMEOUT_MS` + the 5 s backstop, so this should not happen (DEP
         --R20, DEP-R27). If it does, check that a changed setting did no
         -t break that order, or that the container ran out of memory (in
         - AWS, the alarm `scf-ecs-memory` and the task's `stoppedReason`
         -).                                                             
      26 +Exit code 137 means the replica was killed with SIGKILL before 
         +it could finish. Docker Compose waits `stop_grace_period` (40 s
         + for `api-1` and `api-2`) and ECS waits the container's `stopTi
         +meout` (40 s), both above `SHUTDOWN_DRAIN_DELAY_MS` + `SHUTDOWN
         +_TIMEOUT_MS` (32 s) and, with the defaults, above the 37 s back
         +stop too, so this should not happen (DEP-R20, DEP-R27). If it d
         +oes, check that a changed setting did not break that order, or 
         +that the container ran out of memory (in AWS, the alarm `scf-ec
         +s-memory` and the task's `stoppedReason`).

      28  ## Checking a shutdown
      29  
     ...
      36  docker compose start api-1
      37  ```
      38  
      39 -nginx passes a request to the other replica when a connection f
         -ails, and for a POST only if nothing of it was sent yet (DEP-R1
         -5). Stopping one replica therefore loses no request; a request 
         -that was cut off reaches the client as an error and is retried 
         -with the same key.                                             
      39 +nginx passes a request to the other replica when a connection f
         +ails or times out, and for a POST only if nothing of it was sen
         +t yet (DEP-R15). Any other request that meets the stopping repl
         +ica gets an error or a 503, and the client retries it with the 
         +same key, so no movement is lost or applied twice (DEP-AC11).  
      40  
      41  In AWS, ECS deregisters the task from the ALB target group firs
          t, which stops new requests and lets the ALB drain it for 35 s 
          (`deregistration_delay`), then sends SIGTERM. The log group `/s
          cf/api` holds the lines above. The exit code and the reason:
      42

      48  ## Changing the shutdown settings
      49  
      50  - `SHUTDOWN_DRAIN_DELAY_MS` (0 to 60000) and `SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_
          MS` (1 to 120000, not less than `REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS`) are read 
          at startup; an invalid value stops the replica before it listen
          s (SEC-R40).
      51 -- Their sum plus the 5 s backstop must stay below `stop_grace_p
         -eriod` in `compose.yaml` and below `stopTimeout` in the Terrafo
         -rm. The Terraform variables refuse `shutdown_drain_delay_ms` + 
         -`shutdown_timeout_ms` of 40000 or more; raising either beyond t
         -hat means raising `stopTimeout` and `stop_grace_period` too.   
      51 +- Their sum must stay below `stop_grace_period` (40 s) in `comp
         +ose.yaml` and below `stopTimeout` (40 s) in the Terraform (DEP-
         +R20, DEP-R27); the Terraform variables refuse `shutdown_drain_d
         +elay_ms` + `shutdown_timeout_ms` of 40000 or more. Keep the 5 s
         + of the backstop inside that limit too, so the backstop fires b
         +efore the kill. Going beyond means raising `stopTimeout` and `s
         +top_grace_period` as well.

      52  
      53  **nginx** (10-runtime): the upstream lists the replicas' fixed 
          addresses, `server ${API_1_ADDRESS}:3000` and `server ${API_2_A
          DDRESS}:3000`, without `resolve`. nginx passes a failed request
           to the other server only if the upstream's servers have not ch
          anged since the request started (`ngx_http_upstream_get_round_r
          obin_peer` answers busy when they have), and `resolve` changes 
          them when a stopped replica's name stops resolving, which lost 
          the retry in the phase-10 manual test; with fixed addresses the
           servers never change, and a replica that restarts keeps its ad
          dress, so it is found again (DEP-AC10, DEP-AC11). `proxy_connec
          t_timeout 2s` gives up on a replica that is gone, whose address
           drops packets, in time to pass the request to the other one (s
          ection 1.7 of spec 007). `worker_processes 1` keeps the round r
          obin strictly alternating, which DEP-AC09's count of at least 5
           per replica relies on; a comment in the configuration says so,
           because with several workers each keeps its own round-robin po
          sition. `proxy_next_upstream error timeout` with `proxy_next_up
          stream_tries 2`, which nginx applies to a POST only before the 
          request was sent (DEP-R15). The gateway `error_page` returns `a
          pplication/problem+json` with `requestId` = `$request_id` or th
          e client's `X-Request-Id`, and `add_header Retry-After 1 always
          ` (DEP-R16).
      54

  What changed
  - CI (.github/workflows/ci.yml):
    - ci: keeps its name and every existing step. I added npm run infra:validate
      as its own step (the token only avoids GitHub's rate limit on the tflint
      plugin download), uploaded the unit and integration reports, and raised
      the timeout to 20 minutes.
    - e2e: runs npm run test:e2e with a 30-minute timeout and starts no other
      stack. It sets E2E_KEEP_STACK=1 so the scf-e2e containers are still there
      for the logs step on failure, then uploads the e2e report.
    - traceability: runs after ci and e2e, downloads the three reports and runs
      npm run trace -- --require unit,integration,e2e.
    - secret-scan: unchanged.
    - security: runs npm audit --audit-level=high, then trivy fs, config and
      image from the aquasec/trivy:0.75.0 image pinned by digest, failing on
      HIGH and CRITICAL. The image is scanned from a saved file, so the Docker
      socket is never shared.
    - Pins and permissions: every action is pinned to a commit SHA I looked up
      with gh api, with the version as a comment. Permissions stay contents: 
      read, npm is cached, and there is no deploy job.
  - Dockerfile: the runtime stage deletes npm, npx, corepack and yarn in the
    same RUN as npm ci --omit=dev. Seed, token and reconcile stay on the tools
    image. I recorded your approval in plan 000 §11 under DEP-R20, and in
    plan 008.
  - Terraform: I added one #trivy:ignore:AVD-AWS-0053 on the ALB, which is meant
    to be public (DEP-R25), with the reason in a comment. trivy flagged nothing
    else.
  - Dependabot: npm and github-actions now skip semver-major updates, and
    @types/node is held below 25 to match .nvmrc.
  - SEC-AC29 and SEC-AC36: the tests now read the Terraform as well.
    test/support/deployment.ts follows each var.* from the module to the root
    default.
    - The AWS pool budget comes out at 6 × 11 + 10 = 76 < 97.
    - The AWS request timeout is 25000 ms against the ALB's 60 s.
    - Changed copies of the Terraform make each check fail, so they don't pass
      on hard-coded values.

  Things you should know
  - WAF answers 403, not 429: in AWS the WAF blocks with 403 and no problem
    body, while SEC-R01 asks the load balancer for 429 /problems/rate-limited.
    This came from 12.1, and I didn't change it.
  - Deployment surge: a rollout can briefly run twice the tasks
    (deployment_maximum_percent 200). That's over the SEC-AC29 count, which uses
    the 6-task autoscaling maximum, though RDS Proxy still caps the real
    database connections.

      108  ### 1.6 Rate limits
      109  
      110  - **Per IP, locally.** nginx applies `limit_req` on a zone key
           ed by `$binary_remote_addr` with `rate=${RATE_LIMIT_IP_RPS}r/s
            burst=${RATE_LIMIT_IP_BURST} nodelay` and `limit_req_status 4
           29`: requests within the burst pass at once and the excess is 
           refused at once, not queued, so a client gets a prompt 429 ins
           tead of latency. The nginx configuration template is rendered 
           from the environment by the `envsubst` step of the nginx image
           . The 429 is answered by an `error_page 429` location that ret
           urns the problem details of SEC-R01, with `requestId` set to n
           ginx's request id and `add_header Retry-After 1 always`, so a 
           client sees the same shape from nginx as from the service.
      111 -- **Per IP, in AWS.** An AWS WAF rate-based rule aggregated by
          - client IP (SEC-R45). WAF counts over fixed evaluation windows
          - of 1 to 10 minutes and allows no burst, so the rule uses a 1-
          -minute window with a limit of 60 × `RATE_LIMIT_IP_RPS`; nginx'
          -s per-second limit with a burst stays the local stand-in. The 
          -rule is checked by the CI step `npm run infra:validate`, added
          - in phase 12-infra, which runs `terraform validate` and a poli
          -cy check of the plan (SEC-AC35).                              
      111 +- **Per IP, in AWS.** An AWS WAF rate-based rule aggregated by
          + client IP (SEC-R45). WAF counts over fixed evaluation windows
          + of 1 to 10 minutes and allows no burst, so the rule uses a 1-
          +minute window with a limit of 60 × `RATE_LIMIT_IP_RPS`; nginx'
          +s per-second limit with a burst stays the local stand-in. The 
          +rule's block action answers as close to SEC-R01 as WAF allows:
          + status 429, the header `Retry-After: 60`, the rule's window, 
          +and a custom response body of content type `APPLICATION_JSON` 
          +holding the `type` `/problems/rate-limited`, the `title`, the 
          +`status` 429 and the `detail` of the nginx answer. WAF cannot 
          +add a `requestId` or an `X-Request-Id` header, nor send the me
          +dia type `application/problem+json`, so that body is `applicat

      112  - **Per user.** A fixed window per user that starts with the u
           ser's first request: a Redis counter created with a TTL of `RA
           TE_LIMIT_USER_WINDOW_S` and incremented atomically, one round 
           trip per request and one count across replicas. `Retry-After` 
           is the counter's remaining TTL rounded up to whole seconds, at
            least 1 (SEC-R03).
      113  - **Redis failure.** The per-user limit fails open (SEC-R06): 
           each command has a timeout of `REDIS_COMMAND_TIMEOUT_MS`, a fa
           iled check lets the request through, and the service logs one 
           `warn` line on the transition to unavailable and one `info` li
           ne on recovery instead of one line per request. The service st
           arts while Redis is down and readiness ignores Redis, so a Red
           is outage never takes the API down.
      114  - **Test suites.** Integration tests of the other specs build 
           the app with `RATE_LIMIT_USER_MAX` "1000000"; the ACs of this 
           spec use the values they name and the defaults of section 1.2 
           otherwise. The e2e suite uses the defaults: SEC-AC04 uses its 
           own user C9, SEC-AC01 runs alone and after every other e2e AC,
            and the load test (SYS-AC17) spreads its deposits over 10 ope
           rator identities, about 7 requests per second each, and its cu
           stomer requests over the 1000 customers of its account pairs (
           SEC-R09).

      129  - **Role settings.** A migration run by the owner role sets `s
           tatement_timeout` and `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` wi
           th `ALTER ROLE <runtime role> SET ...`, so local, CI and AWS g
           et the same values; the owner role is granted the right to alt
           er the runtime role. `statement_timeout` (5 s) is above both l
           ock waits, so a lock wait always ends as SQLSTATE 55P03, never
            57014; `idle_in_transaction_session_timeout` (10 s) ends a tr
           ansaction left open by a defect. The reconcile and cleanup scr
           ipts raise `statement_timeout` to 600000 ms (10 minutes) for t
           heir own transaction by calling `app.set_statement_timeout(ms 
           integer)`, a function of the same kind as the lock-timeout fun
           ction that accepts 1 to 3600000 ms (SEC-R29).
      130  - **Lock-timeout function.** `app.set_lock_timeout(ms integer)
           `, `SECURITY INVOKER`, checks 1 ≤ ms ≤ 60000 and runs `set_con
           fig('lock_timeout', ms || 'ms', true)`; only the runtime role 
           may execute it (SEC-R31). That RDS Proxy pins a connection on 
           `SET` and `set_config` statements but not on function calls is
            taken from its documentation and recorded in [ADR-0019](../..
           /docs/adr/0019-timeout-layers-and-rds-proxy.md), to be re-chec
           ked when RDS Proxy is introduced in phase 12-infra.
      131  - **Statement timeout.** A statement cancelled by `statement_t
           imeout` is a transient overload like a lock timeout, and the r
           equest has already used most of its time, so it is answered 50
           3 and not retried in process (SEC-R32). At the request timeout
            the 503 is answered first; the service starts no further stat
           ement, and the statement in flight runs until it ends, at most
            until `statement_timeout`, before the transaction is rolled b
           ack (SEC-R33). The service never cancels a statement from anot
           her connection.

      131  ## Request path
      132  
      133  1. The client resolves the domain to the ALB and opens TLS 1.2
            or 1.3 on 443; a request on 80 gets a 301 to HTTPS.
      134 -2. AWS WAF evaluates the request: the per-IP rate rule, then t
          -he two managed rule groups. A blocked request ends here with a
          - 403.                                                         
      134 +2. AWS WAF evaluates the request: the per-IP rate rule, then t
          +he two managed rule groups. A request over the per-IP limit en
          +ds here with a 429, one a managed rule group blocks with a 403
          +.                                                             
      135  3. The ALB drops invalid header fields, appends the client's a
           ddress to `X-Forwarded-For`, its default (nginx replaces the h
           eader instead, SEC-R19), and forwards the request over HTTP to
            a healthy task in either zone. The service trusts `X-Forwarde
           d-For` only from the public subnets, where the ALB's nodes are
            (`TRUSTED_PROXY_CIDRS`), and takes the rightmost address that
            is not one of them: the one the ALB appended, so any value th
           e client sent is ignored (SEC-R18).
      136  4. The task authenticates the token, applies the per-user limi
           t in Redis (failing open without it) and the checks of section
            1.3 of spec 007, then runs the movement in one database trans
           action through RDS Proxy, which hands the transaction a databa
           se connection and takes it back at commit.
      137  5. The answer returns the same way. Logs go to CloudWatch Logs
            through the endpoint; nothing leaves the VPC except the respo
           nse.

      41  ## What to do
      42  
      43 -- **Pool exhausted.** `scf_db_pool_connections{state="waiting"}
         -` stays above 0 and `scf_db_pool_acquire_timeouts_total` rises.
         - Short bursts queue in arrival order and drain without a 5xx (S
         -EC-R38); a steady excess needs more capacity. Raising `DB_POOL_
         -MAX` must keep replicas × (`DB_POOL_MAX` + 1) + 10 below the da
         -tabase's `max_connections` − `superuser_reserved_connections` i
         -n every deployment: 2 × 11 + 10 = 32 < 97 in `compose.yaml`; in
         - AWS the maximum task count (`max_tasks`, 6) times (`db_pool_ma
         -x` + 1) goes to RDS Proxy, which uses at most 90% of the instan
         -ce's connections (SEC-R36, DEP-R29). The unit test of SEC-AC29 
         -fails when a definition breaks the budget.                     
      43 +- **Pool exhausted.** `scf_db_pool_connections{state="waiting"}
         +` stays above 0 and `scf_db_pool_acquire_timeouts_total` rises.
         + Short bursts queue in arrival order and drain without a 5xx (S
         +EC-R38); a steady excess needs more capacity. Raising `DB_POOL_
         +MAX` must keep replicas × (`DB_POOL_MAX` + 1) + 10 below the da
         +tabase's `max_connections` − `superuser_reserved_connections` i
         +n every deployment: 2 × 11 + 10 = 32 < 97 in `compose.yaml`; in
         + AWS a deployment's surge included, `max_tasks` 6 × `deployment
         +_maximum_percent` 200 / 100 = 12 tasks, 12 × 11 + 10 = 142 < 19
         +7 with the parameter group's `max_connections` of 200; the task
         +s connect to RDS Proxy, which uses at most 90% of the instance'
         +s connections (SEC-R36, DEP-R29). The unit test of SEC-AC29 fai
         +ls when a definition breaks the budget.                        
      44  - **Lock timeouts.** Many movements on the same account at once
          : they queue on its row lock in ascending id order (ADR-0008). 
          Occasional 503s under such contention are expected and retried 
          by the client. If they persist with low traffic, look for a ses
          sion holding a lock: `SELECT pid, state, xact_start, query FROM
           pg_stat_activity WHERE state <> 'idle' ORDER BY xact_start;`.
      45  - **Retries exhausted.** `scf_transaction_retries_total` by `sq
          lstate` shows which conflict. Ordered locking should make deadl
          ocks rare; a sustained rise is a defect to report.
      46  - **Statement timeouts.** A slow statement: check the database'
          s load (the alarm `scf-rds-cpu` in AWS) and `pg_stat_activity`.
           Do not raise `statement_timeout`: it must stay above the lock 
          waits and below `REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` (SEC-R34), and it bounds t
          he clean-up after a request timeout.

      105  | Secrets    | Terraform creates the secrets and their KMS key
            but no secret version; the values are set out of band, by an 
           operator or the pipeline, with `aws secretsmanager put-secret-
           value`, before the rest of the configuration is applied. The s
           ecrets of the owner and runtime roles are JSON objects with th
           e keys `username` and `password`, the format RDS Proxy reads: 
           a task gets its database URL without a password as an environm
           ent variable that Terraform builds from the endpoint the task 
           must use, and the password as the secret `PGPASSWORD` from the
            `password` key, which `pg` reads when the URL holds none. The
            Redis secret is a JSON object with the keys `auth_token` and 
           `url` (`rediss://:<auth_token>@<primary endpoint>:6379`): Terr
           aform reads `auth_token` through an ephemeral `aws_secretsmana
           ger_secret_version` into the replication group's write-only `a
           uth_token_wo`, and the service gets `url` as the secret `REDIS
           _URL`. RDS manages its master password in Secrets Manager (`ma
           nage_master_user_password`), encrypted with the same KMS key. 
           No secret value enters the Terraform state.

      106  | Migrations | The pipeline runs the migration task with `aws 
           ecs run-task` and waits for exit code 0 before it updates the 
           service, as `depends_on` does locally. Migrations are expand-t
           hen-contract, so the running version keeps working while the n
           ext one rolls out (section 1.8 of spec 007). The migration tas
           k connects to the RDS instance's endpoint directly, not throug
           h RDS Proxy, from a security group that only it and the bootst
           rap task use: node-pg-migrate holds a session advisory lock fo
           r the whole run, which would pin its connection behind the pro
           xy (ADR-0019). Its URL sets `sslmode=verify-full` and `sslroot
           cert=/app/dist/certs/rds-global-bundle.pem`, AWS's public RDS 
           CA bundle, committed as `certs/rds-global-bundle.pem` and copi
           ed into `dist/certs/` by `npm run build`, because the instance
           's certificate is signed by the RDS CA, which Node does not tr
           ust by default; RDS Proxy presents a publicly trusted certific
           ate, so the service's URL needs no `sslrootcert`.

      107  | Bootstrap  | Once, before the first migration task, an opera
           tor runs the bootstrap task with `aws ecs run-task`. `node dis
           t/cli/bootstrap-roles.js`, connected to the instance as the RD
           S master user the way the migration task connects, creates the
            owner role and the runtime role, sets their passwords to the 
           values of their own secrets, grants the runtime role to the ow
           ner role and makes the owner role the owner of the database, a
           s `docker/postgres/init/01-databases.sql` does locally (sectio
           n 1.3 of spec 002, ADR-0018). In AWS each role holds only what
            the migrations and the service need: the owner role `scf_owne
           r` has `LOGIN`, to connect, and `CREATEROLE`, because on Postg
           reSQL 16 only a role with `CREATEROLE` and the admin option on
            `scf_app` may run the `ALTER ROLE scf_app IN DATABASE ... SET
           ` of the first migration (SEC-R29), and none of `SUPERUSER`, `
           CREATEDB`, `REPLICATION` and `BYPASSRLS`; locally it also has 
           `CREATEDB`, only because the tests create scratch databases. T
           he runtime role `scf_app` has `LOGIN` only. The grant is `GRAN
           T scf_app TO scf_owner WITH ADMIN TRUE, INHERIT FALSE, SET FAL
           SE`: the admin option for that `ALTER ROLE`, and neither inher
           itance nor `SET ROLE`, so the owner role never acts with the r
           untime role's privileges. The owner role owns the database, so
            the migrations can create the schema `app` and tables in `pub
           lic`. The bootstrap computes each password's SCRAM-SHA-256 ver
           ifier itself, so no plaintext password reaches the server, and
            sends the verifier only as a bind parameter, never in a state
           ment's text, so a server that logs DDL statements never logs i
           t. It never prints or logs a password, a secret, a verifier or
            its URL. Running it again creates nothing and sets the passwo
           rds to their secrets' current values, so it also applies a rot
           ation. The master credentials are used by nothing else. |

✻ Baked for 51s · done 3:49 AM

❯ /audit                                                                        
  ⎿  Phase 12-infra review: Terraform, CI, runbooks, OpenAPI and config changes

     Scope: commit 21015ef compared with main, plus the uncommitted and untracked changes.

     Medium

     1. RDS Proxy borrow timeout is unset, so the timeout layers are out of order in AWS
     - Where: infra/terraform/modules/database/main.tf:180
     - What is wrong: connection_pool_config does not set connection_borrow_timeout, so it takes AWS's default of 120 s. That is how long the proxy holds a statement while it waits for a database connection. No setting in the service bounds that wait:
       - statement_timeout does not start, because no backend connection exists yet.
       - DB_POOL_ACQUIRE_TIMEOUT_MS only bounds the local pool.
       - So a database-side wait (120 s) sits above REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS (25 s), the ALB idle timeout (60 s) and SHUTDOWN_TIMEOUT_MS (30 s).
     - How to trigger it: fail over the RDS instance, or saturate the proxy's 180 backend connections. The first statement of each transaction (BEGIN or the key insert) then waits at the proxy for up to 120 s.
     - Impact:
       - The service answers 503 at 25 s, but each stuck pool client stays busy until the proxy gives up. The other requests then fail with PoolAcquireTimeout.
       - A task stopped during this wait exits 1 with work cut off.
       - docs/deployment/aws.md:211 says "During the failover, statements fail". In fact they stall at the proxy.
       - Money stays correct, because the SEC-R33 rollback still applies.
     - Fix: set connection_borrow_timeout well below the request budget (for example 5 s, in line with the lock and pool waits). Add it to the timeout table of spec 007 section 1.7, ADR-0019 and docs/runbooks/timeouts-and-503.md, and correct the failover paragraph. This needs the owner's approval.
     - AC: missing AC (SYS-R35 and SEC-R34 ordering).

     2. Only the Terraform defaults are checked against the connection budget
     - Where: infra/terraform/variables.tf:93 (max_tasks) and :99 (db_pool_max)
     - What is wrong: these variables have no validation block. The SEC-AC29 test (test/unit/deployment/pool-budget.test.ts) only checks the defaults, through resolveTerraform, which reads the root variable's default. Yet docs/runbooks/timeouts-and-503.md:43 says "The unit test of SEC-AC29 fails when a definition breaks the budget". For comparison, request_timeout_ms, alb_idle_timeout_seconds and shutdown_timeout_ms do have validations.
     - How to trigger it: set max_tasks = 10 in the environment's tfvars file. At deployment surge that is 20 × 11 + 10 = 230, above the 197 usable connections and the proxy's 180. terraform validate, checkov and the unit test all still pass.
     - Impact: at peak scale, during a rollout, connections queue at the proxy (see #1) and requests answer 503.
     - Fix: add a validation on db_pool_max along the lines of var.max_tasks * 2 * (var.db_pool_max + 1) + 10 < 197. The 2 is deployment_maximum_percent / 100, ideally taken from a shared local or variable. Also check max_tasks >= min_tasks, and correct the runbook sentence.
     - AC: SEC-AC29 (SEC-R36).

     Low

     3. Two places still give the old connection count
     - Where: docs/deployment/aws.md:68 and specs/008-deployment/spec.md:103 (Service row)
     - What is wrong: both still say "With DB_POOL_MAX 10, 6 tasks hold at most 66 connections to RDS Proxy". variables.tf:100, aws.md:78, spec 007 section 1.9 and the runbook now give 12 tasks and 132 connections, counting deployment surge.
     - How to trigger it: read either document.
     - Impact: the spec contradicts itself.
     - Fix: update both sentences to the surge figures. Spec 008 text needs the owner's approval.
     - AC: SEC-AC29 (docs drift).

     4. The OpenAPI document promises problem details for load balancer errors that the ALB does not send
     - Where: src/platform/http/api-reference.ts:15-22, :108 and src/platform/http/docs.ts:59
     - What is wrong: the document says every operation's 502, 503 and 504 come "from the load balancer" as application/problem+json, with /problems/upstream-unavailable, Retry-After: 1 and X-Request-Id. In AWS, the ALB's own 502/503/504 are text/html and carry none of those. aws.md records the WAF differences (429, 403) but not this one.
     - How to trigger it: in AWS, a request reaches a task that dies, or no target is healthy.
     - Impact: a client that parses the body as problem details fails. Retries still work, because the retry policy keys on the status.
     - Fix: add the ALB difference to the "differences from the local stack" paragraph of aws.md, and to the WHEN text of the type ("locally from nginx; in AWS the ALB answers without a problem body").
     - AC: DEP-R16 (docs drift).

     5. "One not stored committed nothing" is not true for timeouts and gateway errors
     - Where: src/platform/http/docs.ts:59
     - What is wrong: the sentence "one not stored committed nothing, so a retry with the same key runs the request again" contradicts the cases the same document lists below it: a 503 at the request timeout after COMMIT was sent, and the gateway errors. In both, the movement may have committed, and a retry gets the stored response.
     - How to trigger it: read /docs/json.
     - Impact: wrong client guidance only. Behaviour is safe, because the retry with the same key replays the stored response.
     - Fix: add "except a request timeout after COMMIT and a gateway error, whose outcome a retry with the same key reveals".
     - AC: SEC-R33 and IDM (docs drift).

     6. Terraform does not enforce the service's other shutdown rules
     - Where: infra/terraform/variables.tf:133-142
     - What is wrong: the validation accepts shutdown_drain_delay_ms + shutdown_timeout_ms up to 39999 ms. Two of the service's own rules are not checked:
       - The 5 s backstop (src/main.ts:7) then fires after the 40 s stopTimeout.
       - shutdown_timeout_ms >= request_timeout_ms (SEC-R35) is not validated.
     - How to trigger it: either of these settings is accepted by terraform validate:
       - shutdown_timeout_ms = 36000: ECS sends SIGKILL (exit 137) before the backstop.
       - request_timeout_ms = 30000 with shutdown_timeout_ms = 25000: every task refuses its configuration at startup, and the circuit breaker rolls the deployment back.
     - Impact: the mistake is only caught at deploy time.
     - Fix: validate the sum plus 5000 < 40000 and shutdown_timeout_ms >= request_timeout_ms.
     - AC: DEP-R27, SEC-R35.

     7. Runbooks tell AWS operators to run a reconcile they cannot run
     - Where: docs/runbooks/shutdown.md:22 and docs/runbooks/timeouts-and-503.md:47
     - What is wrong: both say "Confirm with npm run reconcile", including for AWS incidents. In AWS there is no way to run it:
       - no reconcile task definition;
       - the runtime image now has no npm and no dist/cli/reconcile.js;
       - the proxy and RDS security groups admit no operator host.
     - How to trigger it: an AWS on-call follows either runbook.
     - Impact: the runbook step cannot be executed in AWS.
     - Fix: state that reconcile is local or CI only for now, or document how to run it in AWS.
     - AC: missing AC (docs drift).

     8. The SEC-AC29 and SEC-AC36 test helper quietly falls back to defaults
     - Where: test/support/deployment.ts:750
     - What is wrong: terraformApiConfig drops any environment entry whose expression does not resolve to a literal. loadConfig then uses the default.
     - How to trigger it: change the api container to { name = "REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS", value = tostring(var.request_timeout_ms + 0) }. SEC-AC36 then checks 25000 instead of the real value.
     - Impact: the test can pass while not checking the deployed value.
     - Fix: throw when an entry naming REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS, DB_POOL_MAX or a timeout cannot be resolved.
     - AC: SEC-AC29, SEC-AC36 (tests).

     Deferred, already planned: moving the specs to Implemented and tracking docs/traceability.md (13-final-review); Prometheus scraping through an ADOT sidecar (out of scope, aws.md Observability); the two-user rotation of the runtime role's password (follow-up in deploy-and-migrate.md); a NAT gateway with an egress allow-list for future internet calls (spec 008 section 1.7).

     - Spec conformance: no findings. Every touched AC has a test with its ID, or a CI-level policy for the ci ACs, and all tasks are ticked.
     - Money: no findings. No amount handling changed in this phase.
     - Transactions and concurrency: no findings. The service sends no SET or advisory lock through the proxy.
     - Idempotency: no findings in behaviour. The only idempotency-related item is the wording in #5.
     - Security: no findings. PGPASSWORD is redacted, the bootstrap sends verifiers only as bind parameters, and the IAM, security groups and secrets are least privilege.

     Not verified: the uncommitted change to infra/policies/sec_ac35_waf_rate_limit.yaml depends on checkov reading the WAF body's JSON string as an object. The transcript does not show npm run infra:validate passing after that change. Run it before shipping. The infra/terraform/modules/secrets/ folder was denied to me by the permission settings, so I did not review it.

  record them in plan 000 §11.                                                  
  1. Set connection_borrow_timeout = 5 on RDS Proxy. When it expires, the proxy 
  answers SQLSTATE 08000 "Timed-out waiting to acquire database connection"     
  (the PostgreSQL table of AWS's RDS Proxy troubleshooting page), which the     
  service answers 500 today: classify a 08000 like a pool acquire timeout, 503  
  /problems/service-unavailable with Retry-After: 1, and check that the pool    
  client is released or destroyed afterwards. Prove it with a unit test that    
  names a new or extended AC in spec 007. Add the borrow timeout to the timeout 
  table of spec 007 section 1.7 and to the 503 causes, add a dated note to      
  ADR-0019, update docs/runbooks/timeouts-and-503.md and the API reference's    
  503, and correct the failover paragraph of docs/deployment/aws.md.

  defaults: max_tasks >= min_tasks, and max_tasks × surge × (db_pool_max + 1) + 
  10 < max_connections − 3, with the 200% surge and the 200 max_connections     
  each defined once and used by both the validation and the resources. Keep     
  SEC-AC29 reading the values from the Terraform, and fix the runbook sentence. 
  3. Update the 66-connection sentences in docs/deployment/aws.md and the       
  Service row of spec 008 to the surge figures.

  problem body; the retry policy keys on the status.

  timeout after COMMIT and a gateway error, whose outcome a retry with the same

  that names DB_POOL_MAX or a timeout cannot be resolved, with a test.

      209  ### Database failover
      210  
      211 -RDS promotes the standby, usually within one to two minutes, a
          -nd RDS Proxy reconnects to it without the service changing its
          - endpoint. During the failover, statements fail and readiness 
          -answers 503: requests answer 503 with `Retry-After`, a movemen
          -t in flight rolls back and its key row is released, and client
          -s retry with the same key. The ALB checks liveness, so the tar
          -gets stay healthy and ECS replaces no task: they answer 503 un
          -til the database is back, and serve again without a restart (s
          -ection 1.6 of spec 008, ADR-0014). Losing the instance without
          - a standby is recovered from the automated backups to a point 
          -in time within 7 days.                                        
      211 +RDS promotes the standby, usually within one to two minutes, a
          +nd RDS Proxy reconnects to it without the service changing its
          + endpoint. During the failover, RDS Proxy holds each new trans
          +action's first statement for at most its `connection_borrow_ti
          +meout` of 5 s, then answers SQLSTATE 08000, and readiness answ
          +ers 503: requests answer 503 with `Retry-After` (SEC-R49), the
          + connection that met the timeout is destroyed rather than reus
          +ed, a movement in flight rolls back and its key row is release
          +d, and clients retry with the same key. The ALB checks livenes
          +s, so the targets stay healthy and ECS replaces no task: they 
          +answer 503 until the database is back, and serve again without
          + a restart (section 1.6 of spec 008, ADR-0014). Losing the ins
          +tance without a standby is recovered from the automated backup
          +s to a point in time within 7 days.

      213  ### Loss of Redis
      214  
      215  The per-user rate limit fails open: each Redis command times o
           ut after `REDIS_COMMAND_TIMEOUT_MS` (100 ms), the request is s
           erved, and the service logs one warning on the transition and 
           one line on recovery (SEC-R06). Readiness ignores Redis, so no
            task is taken out of the ALB. Money movements never touch Red
           is, so they stay correct; the per-IP limit of AWS WAF still ap
           plies. On a node failure ElastiCache promotes the replica and 
           the service reconnects.

         -MAX` must keep replicas × (`DB_POOL_MAX` + 1) + 10 below the da

         -n every deployment: 2 × 11 + 10 = 32 < 97 in `compose.yaml`; in
         - AWS a deployment's surge included, `max_tasks` 6 × `deployment
         -_maximum_percent` 200 / 100 = 12 tasks, 12 × 11 + 10 = 142 < 19

      44  - **Lock timeouts.** Many movements on the same account at once
          : they queue on its row lock in ascending id order (ADR-0008). 
          Occasional 503s under such contention are expected and retried 
          by the client. If they persist with low traffic, look for a ses
          sion holding a lock: `SELECT pid, state, xact_start, query FROM
           pg_stat_activity WHERE state <> 'idle' ORDER BY xact_start;`.
      45  - **Retries exhausted.** `scf_transaction_retries_total` by `sq
          lstate` shows which conflict. Ordered locking should make deadl
          ocks rare; a sustained rise is a defect to report.
      46  - **Statement timeouts.** A slow statement: check the database'
          s load (the alarm `scf-rds-cpu` in AWS) and `pg_stat_activity`.
           Do not raise `statement_timeout`: it must stay above the lock 
          waits and below `REQUEST_TIMEOUT_MS` (SEC-R34), and it bounds t
          he clean-up after a request timeout.
      47 -- **Request timeouts.** The request ran longer than its whole b
         -udget. Its transaction rolls back once the statement in flight 
         -ends; if its `COMMIT` was already sent, the commit finishes aft
         -er the 503, the outcome is unknown to the client, and a retry w
         -ith the same key gets the stored response (SEC-R33, SEC-AC38). 
         -Nothing needs repair; `npm run reconcile` confirms it ([reconci
         -liation](reconciliation.md)).                                  
      47 +- **Request timeouts.** The request ran longer than its whole b
         +udget. Its transaction rolls back once the statement in flight 
         +ends; if its `COMMIT` was already sent, the commit finishes aft
         +er the 503, the outcome is unknown to the client, and a retry w
         +ith the same key gets the stored response (SEC-R33, SEC-AC38). 
         +Nothing needs repair; locally `npm run reconcile` confirms it (
         +[reconciliation](reconciliation.md)). It runs locally and in CI
         + only: in AWS it has no task yet ([limitations](../deployment/a
         +ws.md#limitations-and-follow-ups)).                            
      48  - **Database down or failing over.** Every request needing the 
          database answers 503 and readiness answers 503; liveness stays 
          200, so neither Docker nor ECS replaces the replicas, and they 
          serve again once the database is back ([database failover](../d
          eployment/aws.md#database-failover)).

           t to the instance directly: the migration and bootstrap tasks.
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                   |
      105  | Secrets    | Terraform creates the secrets and their KMS key
            but no secret version; the values are set out of band, by an 
           operator or the pipeline, with `aws secretsmanager put-secret-
           value`, before the rest of the configuration is applied. The s
           ecrets of the owner and runtime roles are JSON objects with th
           e keys `username` and `password`, the format RDS Proxy reads: 
           a task gets its database URL without a password as an environm
           ent variable that Terraform builds from the endpoint the task 
           must use, and the password as the secret `PGPASSWORD` from the
            `password` key, which `pg` reads when the URL holds none. The
            Redis secret is a JSON object with the keys `auth_token` and 
           `url` (`rediss://:<auth_token>@<primary endpoint>:6379`): Terr
           aform reads `auth_token` through an ephemeral `aws_secretsmana
           ger_secret_version` into the replication group's write-only `a
           uth_token_wo`, and the service gets `url` as the secret `REDIS
           _URL`. RDS manages its master password in Secrets Manager (`ma
           nage_master_user_password`), encrypted with the same KMS key. 
           No secret value enters the Terraform state.                   
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                         
                                                                   |
      106  | Migrations | The pipeline runs the migration task with `aws 
           ecs run-task` and waits for exit code 0 before it updates the 
           service, as `depends_on` does locally. Migrations are expand-t
           hen-contract, so the running version keeps working while the n
           ext one rolls out (section 1.8 of spec 007). The migration tas
           k connects to the RDS instance's endpoint directly, not throug
           h RDS Proxy, from a security group that only it and the bootst
           rap task use: node-pg-migrate holds a session advisory lock fo
           r the whole run, which would pin its connection behind the pro
           xy (ADR-0019). Its URL sets `sslmode=verify-full` and `sslroot
           cert=/app/dist/certs/rds-global-bundle.pem`, AWS's public RDS 
           CA bundle, committed as `certs/rds-global-bundle.pem` and copi
           ed into `dist/certs/` by `npm run build`, because the instance
           's certificate is signed by the RDS CA, which Node does not tr
           ust by default; RDS Proxy presents a publicly trusted certific
           ate, so the service's URL needs no `sslrootcert`.

      21  ### Option A: Ordered layers, timeouts on the role, lock timeou
          ts through a transaction-local SQL function
      22  
      23  - **Pros:**
      24 -  - Each layer gives up before the layer outside it: lock waits
         - < statement timeout < request timeout < load balancer (section
         - 1.1 of spec 007), and the service refuses to start with a conf
         -iguration that breaks that budget (SEC-R35).                   
      24 +  - Each layer gives up before the layer outside it: lock waits
         + < statement timeout < request timeout < load balancer (section
         + 1.1 of spec 007), and the service refuses to start with a conf
         +iguration that breaks that budget (SEC-R35). (Update 2026-10-09
         +, phase 12-infra: in AWS one more layer waits between the pool 
         +and PostgreSQL, RDS Proxy's `connection_borrow_timeout`, set to
         + 5 s instead of its default of 120 s; its SQLSTATE 08000 is ans
         +wered 503 and its connection destroyed, SEC-R49, section 1.1 of
         + spec 007.)                                                    
      25    - `statement_timeout` and `idle_in_transaction_session_timeou
          t` are set on the runtime role with `ALTER ROLE ... SET` from a
           migration (SEC-R29), so every session starts with them and the
           service sends no `SET`.
      26    - The lock timeouts go through a small SQL function, `app.set
          _lock_timeout(ms)`, that calls `set_config('lock_timeout', ...,
           true)` (SEC-R31): the value is transaction-local, so it ends w
          ith the transaction and never reaches another request.
      27    - According to AWS, calling a stored function does not pin (s
          ee Decision).

⏺ All eight audit findings are fixed, and the four commands pass. npm run check
  runs 366 unit tests and npm run test:integration runs 335; npm run 
  infra:validate finds nothing; npm run trace covers all 226 ACs. Nothing is
  committed.

  1. RDS Proxy borrow timeout:
     - connection_borrow_timeout = 5 is set on the proxy.
     - SQLSTATE 08000 now becomes a new ProxyBorrowTimeout error, answered 503
       /problems/service-unavailable with Retry-After: 1. It isn't retried, and
       nothing more is sent on that connection, not even ROLLBACK; the
       connection is destroyed, both in the transaction runner and in reads.
     - The new SEC-R49 and SEC-AC39 in spec 007 are proven by unit tests: a
       failed BEGIN, a failed later statement, and a failed read.
     - The timeout table you asked to change in section 1.7 is actually in
       section 1.1 of spec 007; the borrow-timeout row went there. The error
       catalogue got its row too.
     - ADR-0019 has a dated note. The runbook, the 503 text in the API docs, and
       aws.md's database section and failover paragraph are updated.
  2. Connection budget in Terraform:
     - deployment_maximum_percent and db_max_connections are now root variables,
       each defined once and used by both the resources and the validation.
     - max_tasks is validated against min_tasks and against the budget.
     - SEC-AC29 still reads every value from the Terraform; the changed copies
       now edit the variable defaults.
     - The runbook sentence is corrected.
  3. Old connection count: aws.md and the Service row of spec 008 now say 12
     tasks and 132 connections.
  4. ALB errors: the OpenAPI text and aws.md's list of differences (now four)
     say the ALB's own 502, 503 and 504 come as text/html without a problem
     body, and that the retry policy keys on the status.
  5. "One not stored committed nothing": now names the two exceptions, a 503 at
     the request timeout after COMMIT and a gateway error, whose outcome a retry
     with the same key reveals.
  6. Shutdown rules: Terraform now rejects a drain delay + shutdown timeout + 5
     s that isn't below stop_timeout_seconds, and a shutdown_timeout_ms below
     request_timeout_ms. The 40 s stopTimeout is now a variable too, and the
     stopTimeout policy still passes on it.
  7. Reconcile in AWS: both runbooks say npm run reconcile runs locally and in
     CI only. aws.md has a new "Limitations and follow-ups" section with the
     reconcile task as the follow-up.
  8. Test helper: it throws when a budget variable of the api container can't be
     read, whether DB_POOL_MAX, a timeout or the drain delay, including an
     entry written in a shape it doesn't parse. A test covers three cases.

  I checked the new Terraform validations with terraform console on a scratch
  copy: these settings are each rejected with the right message, and the
  defaults pass.
  - max_tasks=10
  - max_tasks=1
  - db_max_connections=100
  - shutdown_timeout_ms=36000
  - request_timeout_ms=30000 with shutdown_timeout_ms=25000

  No repository test runs these validations, because a unit test can't evaluate
  Terraform.

  All the approvals are recorded in plan 000 §11.

  I didn't rerun the e2e suite after these changes, so reports/vitest-e2e.json
  is from the earlier run. CI's e2e job will run it on push.
